I am informed that today's meeting cannot be attended by the following Members: Roberto Battelli, Ljubo Germič, Jožef Horvat by 3 p.m., Franz Pukšič, Villa Rezman, Dr Mitja Slavinec after 10 p.m. and Mr Janko Weber. We're moving on to the 19th, 20th. IN 21. Period of the day - First defence proposal of the law on leasings, proposal of the law on lovemaking in lands, proposal of the law on financing of lands. The draft laws were submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration. I give the floor to the representative of the Government for further explanation of the bill proposals. Dr. Ivan Žagar, Minister without a license, in charge of local self-government and regional policy, please, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u2	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Dear Members and honourable Members! Today's general discussion of the package of provincial legislation is aimed at the most important internal policy issue on the establishment of landscapes in Slovenia at this time. We are therefore starting a process which means the final implementation of the constitutional designs of a state regime based on decentralisation of power. The Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia provided for the establishment of local self-government at two levels, with two types of local communities, municipalities and provinces. Preparations for the establishment of the provinces began with preparations for the establishment of the congregations, that is, more than ten years ago. The establishment of the provinces was explicitly foreseen in the Strategy of the Republic of Slovenia for the Integration of the European Union. However, the establishment of the provinces was questionable from the outset, for the first time because, under Article 143 of the Constitution, municipalities were to be integrated into the provinces, meaning that they were to be the founders of the province of the congregation. Secondly, because the question is always being asked whether the Republic of Slovenia must also be divided internally into provinces according to its territorial scope. The need for establishing landscapes is indisputable today and can be illustrated, for example, by two indicators. At the level of local self-government, i.e. regional, provincial, we are currently conducting municipalities with a good 5% gross product, generally successful and effective. However, with the introduction of the provinces, this percentage would increase to 11% and would approach European countries. On the other hand, the Development Hazard Index as a criterion for the development of existing statistical regions shows that the relationship between the most developed central Slovenia and the least developed Slovenian region of Pomurjem is about 1 to 20. This trend without establishing landscapes and decentralisation, which is necessary for more equitable development, cannot be mitigated. This is why the establishment of the provinces in Slovenia is necessary, which I am sure is both professional and political. Amendments to Articles 121, 140 and 143 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia adopted in June 2006 constitute the first step towards the establishment of provinces in Slovenia. Here goes the thanks to the National Assembly, this reference. The new provisions of Articles 121 and 140 of the Constitution have opened the process of decentralisation of public administration by transferring the performance of State tasks to local communities, thereby enforcing the European principle of subsidiarity in determining the level of implementation of State tasks relevant to each individual. The most important is the amendment to Article 143 of the Constitution. On its basis, the provinces obtained the legal status of self-government local communities. They will be established simultaneously, have the same legal status and tasks throughout the country. The next step in the process of establishing provincial self-government is the adoption of laws that will form the basis for the establishment and functioning of the provinces and will allow the decentralisation of government functions and the transfer of important part of public affairs from the state to the provincial level. The proposed three laws, the Law on Provinces, the Law on Regional Financing and the Law on Regional Elections are based on the constitutional principles of local self-government and the principles incorporated into the legal order of the Republic of Slovenia by the European Charter of Local Self-Government. Their content is the legal institution of the provincial self-government, which is, however, the same institution as the local self-government in the municipalities but is not the same. The proposed laws regulate the functional and organisational component of the provincial self-government and form the basis for functional and fiscal decentralisation, the establishment of landscapes, the election of their authorities and the establishment and commencement of the work of the provinces as of 1 January 2009. This is a systemic part of the comprehensive normative framework for the establishment of provincial self-government. Proposals for laws propose a timetable for the preparation and adoption of laws that will further regulate the components of the provincial self-government. The proposed laws will be followed this year by the procedure for determining the area of the provinces. This debate is about to end with a proposal for a law on the establishment of landscapes. At the same time, a law will be proposed on the establishment of electoral units for elections in the first provincial council. The main solutions to the proposed laws are based on the professional basis for the establishment of the provinces created by eminent experts in the field of constitutional and administrative law over the past ten years. In addition, the experience gained in Slovenia in the 12-year development of local self-government at the municipal level has been taken into account. The draft legislation was also widely coordinated here in the National Assembly. The proposal for a law on provinces is based on the principles of provincial self-government, the area of the tasks of the provinces and the way in which powers are transferred to the provinces and the organisation of the provincial authorities and the provincial administration. The law regulates the procedure and means of ensuring the conditions for the entry into operation of the provinces, i.e. spatial, human and material. The draft law defines the province as a city the level of local self-government between the State and the municipalities and as a territorial political administrative community with the position of a legal person with exceptional powers, with its own property and own resources of funding and directly elected representative body and own administration. The work area of the province's tasks is defined in accordance with Article 143 of the Constitution as matters of local importance, matters of regional importance, tasks of State competence delegated to the provinces and to maintenance. Local affairs are of greater importance than tasks that need to be done in the interest and for the benefit of more municipalities in a more cost-effective, efficient way. Although the fundamental principle of the delimitation of powers between the State, the province [[..]] [[Incomprehensible.]] and the municipalities, and by law, it is stipulated that individual matters of wider importance are determined by the province by its decree if the municipalities agree. [[...]] The tasks of regional importance are those of no local importance, but they are not of national importance, of urban importance, of regional importance. Such tasks include in particular the promotion of coherent regional development, economic development, tourism, agriculture, environmental protection and spatial planning, the construction and maintenance of public roads, regional importance, secondary and higher education, social services, the provision of hospital and specialist health care, the provision of public interest in culture at regional level, etc. The provinces will also carry out a certain part of the tasks of the state administration, which are now carried out by administrative units. These are individual tasks of national competence, such as decision-making on legal matters in areas related to the tasks of regional importance, i.e. in the field of economy, agriculture, tourism, hospitality, construction of facilities and intervention into space. Part of the inspection tasks will also be transferred to the provinces. The proposal for a law regulates the gradual introduction of a single administrative system which combines the execution of state and pension administrative tasks at the provincial level and is the only acceptable way for Slovenia, as a small country, to carry out the decentralisation of the country in the long term. It is more rational than a two-tier administrative system, which would have been established if the country had kept even deconcentrated administrative authorities in the provinces. A concrete transfer of tasks to the jurisdiction of the provinces from 1 January 2009 will be by means of the Law on the Transfer of Orders under the jurisdiction of the Provinces, which the Government of the Republic of Slovenia has classified in its 2007 Work Programme and corresponding amendments and additions to the Area Laws. The bill will be submitted to the National Assembly in the autumn and is now in preparation. As we speak of the jurisdiction of the provinces, we must stress that, in accordance with the principles of local self-government as self-governance local communities, they carry out the tasks of their own competence under the Constitution and the law. Functional autonomy means defining its own labour area of the province, which expresses the common needs, interests of its inhabitants and can carry out them independently and has sufficient resources of funding and property to which it can own, with the competent national authorities monitoring only the legality of the work of its authorities. The provincial self-government, in accordance with constitutional principles and the European Charter of Local Self-Government, shall be implemented directly by the provincial councils and their executive authorities and the inhabitants of the province. However, it is also necessary to ensure that the two levels of local self-government are linked, and the law also places the council of municipalities among the provincial authorities. The proposal for a law in accordance with Article 3 of the European Charter on Local Self-Government provides that the highest decision-making body of the province is a provincial council composed of members elected by voters with permanent residence in the province by a proportional electoral system. The determination of the number of the provincial council is left to the province's statute in accordance with the criteria laid down by law for the composition of the provincial council. In the provincial council of the provinces with municipalities in the area where the Italian or Hungarian national communities live, a representative of the national communities will be a member of the provincial council. The province in which the municipalities in which the Roma community lives will have a permanent working body of the Regional Council for Roma Affairs. At the same time, the President of the Regional Council is the President of the Region, who performs the executive function of the province and represents and represents the province. The president of the province shall be elected by the provincial council from among its members. A council of municipalities will be established in the provinces. The Council of Municipalities is the consultative body of the Regional Council, which is composed of mayors of municipalities from the region. The Council of municipalities shall examine and give an opinion on individual matters of general interest and interest to individual or all municipalities. The proposal for a provincial law specifically regulates the relationship between municipal authorities and provincial authorities. The Regional Administration shall manage administrative, professional, acceleration and development tasks as well as tasks related to the provision of public services under regional jurisdiction. It will be organised on a case-by-case basis with regard to the provincial powers of the provincial administrative authorities. The government will lay down by regulation the provision of personnel, spatial and other conditions for the start of the province's work. The bill provides that the personnel core of the provincial administration is to be ensured by the transfer of public servants employed in public administrations. The proposal for a law on the financing of the provinces is based on the starting points that are now real. Therefore, there is no increase in the public financial framework, no new tax resources and as sources of funding we have a completely uneven distribution of tax resources and the provinces must ensure the functioning of the tasks entrusted to them. The Land Financing Act will therefore provide the provinces with an estimated amount of income corresponding to their respective powers and will enable the implementation of its own policy. The bill provides for funding sources for the province. In order to finance the mandatory tasks of the landscapes other than from investments, resources from income and individual excise duties shall be allocated. A country which does not cover the appropriate expenditure with the said resources shall receive financial compensation from the State budget for the financing of the mandatory tasks. For the development needs of the province, 25% of the tax on the income of legal persons paid in the preceding year by an ally established in its territory shall be paid. It is determined that the uneven inflow of these revenues is offset. Thus, the province under this tax may receive a maximum of 150% of the share per capita in each financial year, but not less than 100% of the average amount of the tax on the income of legal persons per capita in the country. This provides all the provinces with a basic resource for carrying out development investment tasks. Other sources of financing for the landscapes are dedicated sources of funding that will need to be specifically defined in sectoral laws, proportionately delegated tasks, such as concessions. The additional resources of the State budget and the European Union fund are intended to co-finance development programmes and more concrete investment projects. The draft law on elections to the province provides for a system of elections to the provincial council, namely electoral law, electoral system, electoral bodies, general elections, early and alternate elections, candidateing, voting and electoral outcomes, and the protection of electoral law. The bill is based on the principles of local self-government based on the constitutional arrangements of the local self-government of the Republic of Slovenia and the principles of the European Charter of Local Self-Government. The main solutions to the law are as follows. Elections in the provincial council shall be carried out through a professional electoral system, ensuring a proportionate distribution of mandates and thus the equal representation of political parties and lists. The province is necessarily divided into constituencies; at least five candidates are elected in the individual constituency. The term of office of the provincial council shall be five years. Dear Members! The establishment of landscapes means the decentralisation of the country, which means the acceleration of more coherent polycentric development, means the provision of comparable conditions for work and life in all parts of Slovenia. The establishment of landscapes means the transfer of an important part of public affairs from the state to the provincial level, at which they are more easily and better aware of the needs in their environment and respond more quickly to them, so that they come closer to people and increase their benefits. In our internal political life, the landscape regime will also bring new quality, and this is a greater responsibility for individual areas for their faster and more balanced development. It also means, according to the saying of Primož Trubar,'state and stay', the situation throughout the territory Slovenia has, so I am confident that today's debate will lead in this direction and also help to improve the proposed legislation within possible frameworks, because I believe that we all want a successful developmental landscape. Thank you for your attention and support.

The introduction of the provinces mainly entails functional and fiscal decentralisation of governance in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. The provinces are thus to carry out their tasks independently, in accordance with the Constitution and the laws for the benefit of their inhabitants, in accordance with the principles of local self-government, i.e. also for members of Hungarian and Italian nationalities. I would not analyse the laws, only from the point of view of the national community would I present the comments we have on this law. In general, I can say that the lawmakers have not worked too hard to put the work and life of the national community into this law, so our concepts of marriage are very, very weak. Just a few comments. The provinces, which will also include national mixed areas, must have a bilingual name. This has never been dreamed of by law-makers. Bilinguality is also used meaningfully in these provinces for signatures and symbols of its own. The provincial administration in the provinces, which cover ethnically mixed areas, operates bilingually. Is this expressed enough in Article 11 of this Law? What tasks will the state delegate to regions related to the protection of national communities and the protection of specific minority rights, especially collective rights? Is there anything else besides education and education and culture in the language of indigenous national communities and the economic basis of indigenous national communities? It is not specified how many representatives of the Italian and Hungarian national communities will be represented in the regional councils. Will this percentage be 10%, as in the municipal councils? Furthermore, the question is whether the regional councilors of the Italian and Hungarian national communities will have an imperative or a representative mandate? The Commission for the National Community should also have jurisdiction among labour bodies in the provinces, including national mixed areas. On a proposal from the President of the province, the Ukrainian Council shall elect one or more Vice-Presidents of the province. I propose that, in the event that the President of the province, which includes national mixed areas, is not a representative of the national community, which he will certainly not, be elected as one of the Vice-Presidents of the Regional Council of the National Community representative elected on the special list of the national community. At the time of the acquisition of public servants from the state administration to the provincial administration, including national mixed areas, the question of the knowledge of Italian and Hungarian or bilingual staff in the provincial administration is raised. The issue of cooperation between the umbrella self-governing national communities and the provinces, including national mixed areas, is also raised. There are also some comments on the draft law on elections in the provinces. The special provincial electoral directory consists of the Italian and Hungarian national communities. It is therefore certain that in elections we must also have this electoral directory by which members of nationalities can choose their representatives in the provincial councils. One of the members of the provincial electoral commission in the provinces, including ethnically mixed areas, is a representative of the Italian or Hungarian national communities. A point should be inserted on the composition of the electoral committees for the election of candidates, members of the national community, into the provincial council. In the constituency-mixed areas, vowels are bilingual. Votes for the election of representatives of the national communities are held in the language of the national community and in the constituents of the national mixed areas, the electoral announcements are bilingual. Does the same apply to voter records? The announcements and minutes for the election of representatives of the national community shall be in the language of the national community. Since there are so many shortcomings in these laws, I cannot support the laws. And that doesn't comfort anyone else to have another, a fourth marriage to put all of this in the right place. This fourth law should be seen first and then voted on. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u5	Thank you. Mr Bojan Kontic will present the position of the Group of Social Democrats. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u6	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Dear Mr. Minister, Secretary of State with colleagues, colleagues and colleagues! In the introduction, I sincerely hope that Friday, 13th, will work well for the establishment of the provinces, including for those who may be a little superstitious. It has been a year since we amended the three articles of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and made it possible to prepare, treat, adopt and implement provincial legislation. The very constructive role of the majority of the opposition and the commitment to regionalism and thus the decentralisation of Slovenia were necessary conditions to ensure the necessary two-thirds majority here in the National Assembly to change the constitution. By that time, we had been informed that the laws of the provinces were ready, but that was not the case. For quite some time, we had to ask both the minister and the president. Government, what is the promised legislation. If we consider the delay, we must admit that Dr. Zhagar and his associates are ready for the agreement and our cooperation. He also took a few of our comments into account in the drafting phase. With the amendment of the Law on the Financing of Landscapes, our reason for opposing further consideration of the package of provincial legislation as a whole has also fallen away. After the Government has adopted and forwarded legislation to the parliamentary procedure, it is our assessment that a substantive debate has begun, which will be put to the vote in favour or against the proposed innovations in the field of local self-government at the end of the process. The content, the degree of complexity and some confusion led us to demand a general discussion, which the Rules of Procedure allow us to do. In setting the agenda for the Social Democrats' Group, we did not support the withdrawal proposal, because we are confident that the same content would meet us in the autumn. In this way, we will use today's debate as a viable debate, which, at least so I hope, will be a good basis for the Minister, the Government and the Coalition to prepare quality amendments for the second reading of these laws. In doing so, I should like to point out that we are looking forward to this in the Group of Social Democrats, and the Minister can also see this as a condition for our continued cooperation and support, the package of legislation and its simultaneous treatment and adoption at the same meeting. So also the Law on the Establishment of Landscapes, which will answer the question of how many provinces, how many large provinces, their territorial scope and also the headquarters of the provinces, and the law on electoral units for the first elections in the provincial councils. Of course, we also expect a so-called technical law, which we consider to be very substantive in our Group of Social Democrats, which will accurately define the tasks that are transferred from country to country. It is probably not unreasonable for us to expect to have enough accurate calculations or simulations of the financing of the provinces by then. We still do not believe that this legislation, this project, will be financially neutral. It is urgent to present the costs on one side and the advantages on the other, which will be reflected in more coherent regional development. We are also expecting a human resources scheme which will deny the anticipated additional employment and thus new costs. In 2004, in the previous mandate, the Ministry of the Interior, led by Mr Rado Bohinc at the time, prepared documents and studies on the provinces in Slovenia 2000-2004. This document found that reforms of local self-government are a constant in all countries, including those with long-standing participation of residents in public affairs. The issue is the distribution of powers between the country and local communities, decentralisation, and resources to finance these local communities, which must provide sufficient resources and financial autonomy. Otherwise, we get disabled local communities and we can only talk about local self-government conditionally. However, local self-government allows the principle of subsidiarity to be respected in practice. In order to effectively implement this principle of subsidiarity, the province is also, according to the European Charter of Regional Self-Government, the most appropriate level of authority. In the midst of the country’s fragmentation into 210 municipalities, which have been well received in their daily lives, we have, in our desire for self-government, additionally centralized the country and at the same time left an empty space between the congregation and the country. And there's something else about the number of landscapes. In the past, we have had several options prepared and also arguments for their implementation. The profession advocated the solution of eight provinces. Dr. Šmidovnik, in an interview with the weekly Mladina, asked why the eight provinces, he replied, quote: "Because the provinces would be stronger. If the landscapes are expected to be a carrier of regional development, it is clear that eight regions in economic, financial, personal, in short any sense, will be stronger", the end of the quote. There are 14 provinces proposed in the works that the Government has forwarded to the public debate. This number was assessed as a political reality as a number that has the necessary support in the National Assembly. In view of the comments and the debate heard so far, the spirit has already escaped from the bottle. It will be very difficult for people who want a new province to explain that the number 14 is still acceptable. And what can happen to us once again when until the vote it was always unclear how many will be created, which will be urban and how many will be new. For the Group of Social Democrats, the number of provinces is not the most important. However, the answer to the question is how closely linked to the question of what the content of these landscapes will be. However, when establishing the provinces, we must clearly have such functional, financial, material, territorial, organisational, including personnel, and a legal picture of the province. A trip to the unknown could be too risky for all of us. In spite of everything in the Group of Social Democrats since its inception, we have advocated a two-step local self-government since the beginning of local self-government. So are the provinces. For many years, in this Parliament, a long-standing parliamentarian, Dr Ciril Ribičič, has sought regionalisation for decentralisation against centralisation. And he listed several arguments in one of his contributions. Let it be allowed to use a few of them to substantiate the fact that we really need the provinces. In the first argument of the diversity of Slovenia he wrote that Slovenia is a country of the provinces. Its characteristic lies in the diversity of its individual parts, both relating to historical extraditions, the degree of development, other characteristics of economic and cultural development, linguistic and other specificities, the breakdown of the territory, the population, etc. Such a diverse country should be organised, regardless of its size, in such a way as to give an expression of particularity in the management of its landscapes in accordance with their specificities. The argument of invalidity of regulation. The reform of local self-government carried out under the new constitutional regime has so far been very one-sided. It restricted itself to the first level of self-government organisation, namely the formation of municipalities and the centralisation of powers that were once exercised by large municipalities as communes and neglected the regionalisation of the state. This is even more urgent today than in the past, as the reform has at least tripled the gap between small municipalities that have lost many powers and centralised state administration. The gap between the congregation and the country is so great that we can talk about the disability of the current organisation. The development element is also important. Regionalisation of the country can contribute to Slovenia's balanced regional development. The differences between the central and peripheral regions have only increased over the last decade. Among other things, because the provinces were not established. Of course, it is possible and must be organised so as not to endanger the position and authority of the congregations and to increase the impact of the congregations on the exercise of the province and the country as a whole. The municipalities, apart from the city’s, today do not perform almost any of the most important functions that would make sense to bring them to the provinces, so that they will have to receive their powers and resources from the state, not from the municipalities. The guarantee that the provinces will not endanger the municipalities is consistent with the principle of subsidiarity as a fundamental principle of the European Charter of Local Self-Government, not only in the relationship between the State and the future provinces, including between the latter and the municipalities. So between the provinces and the municipalities. If I use a European argument. It is not just about taking advantage of the experience of local self-government in Europe and applying the principles to which the European Charter of Local Self-Government and the orientations of the Congress of Local and Regional Authorities of Europe binds us, but it is also about enabling Slovenia to cooperate more effectively and more equally with the regions of neighbouring and other European countries and drawing funds to accelerate regional development from the Structural Funds of the European Union. The available resources are much higher than the cost of a rational regionalisation of the country. These are the arguments written in his contribution by Dr Cyril Ribičič, as I said, a longtime parliamentarian who has sought to decentralise the Republic of Slovenia. Today, we are not particularly aware of the following fundamental answers that need to be answered before the adoption of the entire package of provincial laws, which was also the condition of the Social Democrats in the previous talks between the parties. In fact, it is not yet clear what will happen to the areas of the individual provinces, which has an important impact on their position and ability to carry out the tasks delegated, as these areas will only be known after the current public debate, which takes place in the municipalities and in the professional political public. So far, however, a large number of comments indicate difficulties in determining the areas of the provinces that will satisfy at least most municipalities at the agreed starting point so that the number should not increase beyond 14. It is not yet known what will actually be transferred to the provinces, as this will depend on the adopted, so-called technical law to regulate this as a change to almost 40 laws and other regulations. The working areas of the province's tasks are defined by the fact that in many areas the province assumes a number of tasks of regional importance, in the areas of regional development, environment and space, education and sport, health, economy, transport, culture, work, family and social protection, agriculture and protection and resolution, as defined in Article 13 of the Act, which does not show what these tasks are and to what extent, as this will depend on the amended legislation, this is from the so-called technical law. Therefore, it is not yet possible to predict what resources and personnel will be needed to do so. It would be very unrealistic to adopt a package of provincial legislation without this data. Specific simulations of the necessary means to carry out the tasks of regional importance and of the personnel to be transferred from country to country and necessary to carry out the tasks of the provinces are not prepared. A number of issues relating to the provision of economic and non-economic public services are raised and it is not clear when rules will be adopted to provide by law certain tasks of municipalities and local public economic and non-economic services, which are common areas of several municipalities referred to in Article 12 of this Act. Although the majority concept of the province's organisation by transferring development and administrative functions to the provinces has been adopted, there are still many open questions about the dynamics and way in which administrative functions are transferred. We are not sure that the planned districts are a good journey along with the provinces for the provinces of the amphibians, but in the preparation of the Law on State Administration, which introduces 14 administrative districts and does not overlap with this law. In addition to these basic issues, new dilemmas are being raised in public debates, which will also have to be addressed before the final adoption of the package of provincial laws. These are the direct or indirect elections of the president of the province, the delegated tasks of the country, the technological and economic function of the provinces, the role of the Council of Municipalities and so on. In addition to the Law on the Establishment of the Provinces, the Law on the Financing of the Provinces is the most important and most demanding of the package of provincial legislation. I believe that at the outset all doubts must be resolved as to whether they hold often-mentioned claims about the establishment of the provinces as a public-funded neutral operation. There is no doubt that the new provinces will increase public costs and if we want to ensure their success, it is clear that there will be some additional money needed to do so. It is very naive to expect that national human resources tasks will be transferred to the provinces for the functioning of the provinces and that there will be no new employment and no additional costs by setting up the landscapes as a place to provide other necessary conditions for normal work. But it is not a fundamental problem whether new and higher public financial costs will arise, but whether we will achieve a new development momentum through the establishment of the landscapes and, above all, we will be able to ensure more equitable regional and polycentric development by using our own resources and resources of the European Union. If the expectations of the successful work of the provinces are met, the slightly increased public financial costs will also be met. The main problem is that we are in the dilemma of whether this will be achieved still leaves the Proposal for a Law on the Financing of Landscapes. We do not have enough analysis and data to at least make sure that there are no problems with the functioning of the provinces and, above all, that we can see that the fundamental development role of the provinces will take place. Since the first drafts, the law has undergone a number of changes in the right direction, but we are still convinced that no one in this country can answer with the expected certainty the questions about the necessary means to operate a particular province. The first issue is the funds for the current tasks of the provinces, as set out in the very framework of the Lands Act and should require more than €910 million. These would be financed in accordance with the proposal of the law by the income of the provinces, by a derogation from the part of taxes and by financial compensation, as the revenue from the law of certain shares of income and excise duty is intended to finance the appropriate consumption of the province in which the provision of certain decentralised activities is covered. Therefore, the proportion of the provinces, general taxes provided for by the Constitution or the law, represents their own source of revenue, which has been under discussion for some time now whether in this case it is actually the fiscal independence of the provinces. The answer to this question will certainly have to be found before we decide on the way to finance the provinces, as is now stated in this text that we have in our consideration. It is also undeniable for the Social Democrats that, on the basis of solidarity, it is necessary to provide the necessary resources for the work of all the provinces. But simulations that show that there will be a need for financial alignment in the vast majority of the established provinces remind us of a story about the financing of municipalities that is not good. Indeed, tax resources are highly unevenly distributed across the country and it is therefore difficult to find autonomous tax resources for most of the province, the developmental motivation of those who expect compensation from the country for their work, regardless of the results, is very questionable. The second issue is investment and development programmes, which should also be the basic development function of the provinces, estimated at more than €255 million and financed by income from corporate income tax. This is supposed to be the only autonomous source on which the provinces can count in their development function and which can also be directly affected. These funds are expected to join EU or state budget funds estimated at just under €165 million. Together, some more than €400 million of development funds should be made available, which is not small, but no one can ensure that part of these funds would not be spent to cover the costs of the province's current tasks if the resources from the resources withdrawn were not sufficient. The funding of the tasks transferred is estimated at some over EUR 20 million and is financed under the draft law from the state budget. If I made a few more concrete remarks, I would particularly stress the country's over-the-counter role in decision-making on individual issues of financing for the development of the provinces. Thus, the Government adopts a series of rules and decisions without the participation of the provinces and irrespective of their will, which is very controversial in terms of the autonomy and autonomy of the provinces. Thus, the state or government or the ministries make a very important decision on investments. ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u10	Good afternoon. We agree that the establishment of the provinces in Slovenia is a very responsible task, which requires a thorough reflection and a broad public debate before the establishment of the country. The adoption of a constitutional law amending Articles 121, 140 and 143 of the Constitution created the basis for the drafting of laws on the basis of which power functions will be decentralised and the performance of public affairs will be transferred to the provincial level. The proposed laws define the province as the city level of local self-government between the state and the municipalities. They will be shaped as a new administrative, territorial structure. There will be changes and innovations in the legal and economic field, in the field of state administration and public services, and the impact will also be reflected in the field of municipalities or in the situation of municipalities and populations. For these reasons, we advocate a broad public debate, not only about the seat and the area of the provinces, but also about their role, tasks and funding. In New Slovenia we believe that the rules of the game must be known from the beginning. Therefore, in our view, it is not wrong to discuss the proposed three laws at first reading today. However, it is crucial that we also know the content of the law on the transfer of tasks to the provinces before these laws are passed and the law on the establishment of the provinces and their areas. It's a so-called technical law. Another time. New Slovenia supports such legal solutions to the establishment of the provinces, which will ensure the real decentralisation of Slovenia in accordance with the principles of subsidiarity. Before establishing the landscapes, their role must be known and clear. We need to know exactly what the provinces will do. It is necessary to define which tasks and to what extent they will be carried out in the provinces from 1.1.2009 onwards. We advocate a consistent delegation of tasks already carried out in the ministries and oppose the idea that new tasks are being carried out in the provinces, which have not yet been provided with funding resources. As we know in the New Slovenia Group, the response of ministries is not the most determined. To date, the only Ministry of Education and Sport should clearly define the tasks it intends to delegate to the provinces. The scope of these tasks represents a third of the estimated funding for the implementation of all tasks to be carried out at the provincial level. What about other areas? We know from the congregation’s experience that congregations have often been burdened with new and new tasks and the resources for their implementation have not been provided. With amendments to the Law on Community Financing in 2005, it was finally ensured that municipalities were provided with more financial compensation. In the New Slovenia Group, we consistently advocate the principle of subsidiarity, which provides for an appropriate distribution of powers between different levels. The country, the province and the congregation. In this way, transparency should be ensured when making decisions that should be taken at the citizens' level as close as possible. Third time. We are working towards solutions that will provide a strong development component for the provinces. The provinces must become a generator of coherent regional development. The introduction of landscapes is also important in order to ensure coherent regional development and the greater impact of the population on decision-making. In the New Slovenia Group, we are particularly committed to ensuring that the landscape financing system is set up to help reduce the development differences between the provinces. As we have advocated in the municipalities, it is right that, above all, at the request of Nova Slovenije, the development risk index has been included in the formula for calculating the appropriate consumption. This will provide the provinces with means for regular operation without burdening the resources allocated to the development programmes. Last but not least, this is the case with the European Charter, which provides that financially weaker local communities must be protected by financial compensation procedures or other appropriate measures to remedy the effects of the uneven distribution of possible financial resources and the financial burden they bear. Such procedures or measures shall not reduce the freedom of local authorities to decide on their tasks. The formation of landscapes could make it possible to draw more effectively from European funds, as the preparation and implementation of regional projects will be less difficult. Cross-border cooperation could also be strengthened. Fourth time. New Slovenia will support the establishment of landscapes, which will not require additional burden on Slovenian taxpayers. As is clearly the position of the New Slovenia Group, which advocates consistent subsidiarity and knowledge of precisely defined tasks, it is also clear that in the New Slovenia Group we will support the introduction of the provinces if there is no need for additional burdens on public funds. The transfer of tasks to the provinces should also transfer funds and personnel for their implementation. The introduction of the provinces is a serious milestone in the history of the Slovenian state. We therefore expect the applicant to introduce the provinces so that the taxpayers do not feel it in their pockets. Fifth. New Slovenia advocates the financing of the landscapes in accordance with the principles of public financing and the dowry of the law governing public finances and in accordance with the constitutional provision that the State must provide the province with funds in proportion to the type and extent of the tasks transferred. The financing of the landscapes must comply with the principles of transparency, efficiency and efficiency and financial optimisation of the management of public funds. It is not as important which tax resources will be allocated to the provinces as it is to provide a stable source of funding. Additional funds for the implementation of the development or priority tasks of the provinces may only be used as a debt under the condition that public finances remain in good condition. Six. From a financial and organisational point of view, the provinces must remove and remove administrative obstacles for citizens. In the New Slovenia Group, we want to ensure more appropriate conditions for the development and realization of all the capacities of the population in all areas of life by removing bureaucratic obstacles. This is particularly true for the provinces, which will represent an intermediate level between the country and the municipality. Effective public administration reduces administrative procedures and works in the interests of citizens. In the New Slovenia Group, we note that the creation of provincial administrations is not an opportunity for new jobs, but in administrations it is necessary to re-employ public officials who are already carrying out tasks. Not only must the services we need collected in one place be accessible. In the New Slovenia Group, we strongly oppose the reduction of the availability of services for the population. We cannot agree that the inhabitants of the more remote places would find it more difficult to access services, such as obtaining passports, other documents, certificates and so on, due to the introduction of the provinces. We expect a clear solution to the jurisdiction and tasks of the provinces vis-à-vis existing administrative units to be identified before the adoption of the legislation. We also hope in the New Slovenia Group that the establishment of the provinces will not undermine the task of municipalities. In the New Slovenia Group, we have strongly supported all the reductions and in some cases the abolition of administrative duties. This means removing car stickers, reducing the prices of notary services and so on. Therefore, in the parliamentary group, we will by no means allow the introduction of the provinces indirectly, through taxes, or directly, through administrative and other charges, to cause an increase in the financial burden on the inhabitants. Next, point seven. New Slovenia will take into account the consent of the municipalities or the referendum will of the citizens when establishing the provinces. In the New Slovenia Group, we place the position of an individual in society as a priority. In accordance with the European Charter on Local Self-Government, we take into account that local authorities are one of the main foundations of any democratic regime. We are convinced that this right can be exercised most directly at local level. The European Charter on Local Self-Government provides, inter alia, that the borders of local authorities do not change without prior consultation with the local communities to which they relate, preferably through a referendum where this is permitted by law. That is why we are pleased in our parliamentary group and welcome the Government's decision to publish the proposal for a landscape area by calling for a public debate in the media and to send it to the municipal councils. In the Member State of New Slovenia we will support such a proposal for a law that will take into account the opinion of the municipalities or the referendum will of the citizens. Eight. New Slovenia advocates as few provinces as possible or as many as possible, which, according to the criteria for establishing the provinces, will ensure the optimal functioning of the provinces. And what's to add? What is political reality is now more or less almost known. The New Slovenia Group supports the introduction of the provinces, taking into account the eight points listed above. The position of the New Slovenia Group is similar to that of individual proposals for laws. We will support their adoption if the burden of taxpayers on the introduction of the provinces does not increase and if the tasks to be carried out by the provinces are clearly defined. The Provinces Act is a fundamental law governing the status of the province, the principles of provincial self-government, the labour area and the organisation of provincial authorities and administration. The work area is defined by a loose definition of the tasks to be performed by the provinces. It is a matter of regulating local matters of wider importance, which are common areas of several municipalities. The specific law will set out the tasks of regional importance and delegated tasks to be carried out by the provincial administration. In the New Slovenia Group, we particularly note that accessibility to services for the population must not be reduced, to which we will pay particular attention below. The authorities of the province envisaged by the bill are the provincial council and the president of the province. The Provincial Council shall be composed of 33 to 55 members. We also welcome the decision that the position of the president of the province is not compatible with the function of a Member or other State function, as well as with the function of mayor or mayor. The draft law also defines cooperation with municipalities, other provinces and state authorities. The transitional provisions set out the deadlines for the delegation of administrative tasks. The government will lay down by regulation how to provide for staffing, spatial and other conditions for the entry into operation of the provinces. When transferring tasks and transferring public servants from the authorities of the State Administration and municipalities in the New Slovenia Group, we insist on the parallel transfer of tasks, staff and financial resources for their execution. The basic starting point for the proposed Land Financing Act by the New Slovenia Group is not to increase the taxpayer's tax burden, but to transfer the corresponding share of public funds through the transfer of tasks. In addition, in the New Slovenia Group, we want this law to contribute to reducing the developmental differences between the individual provinces. We believe that the inclusion of the Development Hazard Index in the calculation of the appropriate consumption of the province will help to do so. The index expressing the ratio between the province's development threat index and the weighted arithmetic mean of the country's development hazard index is weighted by 20% points. This is not a new burden on public finances, but a proper distribution of financial resources. These are set for each province as a percentage of the revenue collected, of the income of legal persons, of income and of excise duties, and of other resources. It is important for our parliamentary group, which is working towards more balanced regional development, that these percentages are limited to the population by the average of the country, not more than 50% and not less than 25%. The proposal for a law on the financing of the provinces also sets out the scope of the debt. The scope shall be determined by decree itself, taking into account the Law on Public Finance and the law on the implementation of the budget. The consent of the Ministry of Finance shall be required in cases where the province is indebted to the extent permitted. In the New Slovenia Group, we believe that such a solution is appropriate, as additional indebtedness of the provinces could only be possible if public finances remain in good condition. We are also convinced that long-term debt under the state budget is the cheapest solution for the provinces. The proposal for a law on the financing of landscapes by specific provisions governs the co-financing of the tasks, programmes and investments of the provinces so that, irrespective of other provisions, the State budget landscapes can be provided with funds to co-finance tasks at the level of the appropriations from the European Union. It also provides for the provision of investment financing in the State budget, for the implementation of coherent regional development measures on the basis of a development programme plan, direct budgetary users and in line with the criteria set by ministers up to 100% of the eligible investment costs, of course taking into account the development risk index. The last of the provincial laws is the Law on Elections in the Provinces. The members and members of the provincial councils shall be elected by the electorate and the electorate freely and in secret on the basis of direct, equal and general electoral rights. The mandate of the authorities of the province shall be 5 years. The law also regulates the way in which members of the Italian and Hungarian minorities are elected in the provinces where they live. In each province, one representative of each national community will be elected after the majority electoral system. A proportional electoral system shall apply to elections in the provincial councils. The law stipulates that electoral units shall be formed for elections to the provincial council. These are designed to make one member of the world loved to about the same number of people. The constituency, however, covers the territory of one or more municipalities or the work of municipalities. The size and number of constituencies will be determined by the provincial council for the first elections. The New Slovenia Group points out that we will have to be very careful when adopting this law to create the number and area of individual constituencies. In New Slovenia, we will strive to ensure that all parts of each province are evenly represented so as not to dominate the regional centres at the expense of the rest of the provinces. In the draft law, the proposer proposed a 4% electoral threshold, which would result in the effective functioning of the provincial councils. The distribution of mandates itself is similar to that of the municipal council elections. In Novi Slovenija we support the use of Droop's coefficient in the calculation of direct mandates in the electoral unit. This will allow for the distribution of more mandates already at the level of the electoral unit. The law allows voters among candidates who have been proposed by political parties or by voters themselves to choose the one who trusts him and gives him a preferred vote by signature. The law requires candidates to take equal representation of both sexes into account when creating lists. In the New Slovenia Group, we support the gradual application of the percentage of gender representation, just as it is regulated in the election of members of the National Assembly. Let me conclude, in the New Slovenia Group we estimate that the content of all three laws is acceptable for further consideration. Members of New Slovenia will continue to represent the views presented in the proceedings. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u11	Thank you. Mr Marjan Drofenik will present the position of the Parliamentary Group of the Slovenian People's Party. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u12	Honourable President, Honourable Minister, Members, Members of Parliament, including all those citizens of the Republic of Slovenia who accompany us through the media with a nice greeting! In the Slovenian People's Party, we believe that it is a great success for the Government to decide and put into practice legislation for the establishment of regions. We believe that this move was necessary and that it will also be useful for Slovenia. In this National Assembly, we have shown our intention to regulate the provinces as a second level of local self-government when we appointed the last government, when we also appointed the Minister for Local Self-Government and Regional Development. This direction has been even more clearly supported by amendments to the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, which have so far provided us with an inadequate legal basis for the establishment of regions or landscapes with a better, more appropriate regime that has enabled the drafters to prepare legislation for the establishment of provinces in Slovenia. The Republic of Slovenia has already decided to self-government by adopting a new constitution. The fact is that the concepts of local self-government were different at the time of the formation of the Slovenian Constitution, and the experience of the municipal system in the previous country was also important. Such a situation has also led to the fact that the regulation of the wider self-government communities in the Constitution was inadequate or that did not allow for the actual establishment of the provinces as well as the wider self-government local communities. In the Slovenian People's Party, we have been stressing the importance of local communities and local self-government as an extremely important aspect of a democratic state all the time. For the local community allows people in their territory to regulate those areas of social life that directly relate to the lives of the local population. For the better life of the inhabitants, it is also important to regulate landscapes as well as other levels of local self-government in order to regulate areas beyond the capacity of municipalities. That is why we also supported the amendments to the Constitution, which regulated the status of the provinces and ensured that all areas in Slovenia will be included in the provinces, which, in accordance with the previous existing regime based on voluntary assembly of municipalities, was practically impossible. The establishment of the provinces in Slovenia is necessary. I think there is no longer any doubt about this, even in today's debate over so-called provincial laws. We therefore expect, in particular, to think about which solutions in the proposed laws are questionable, perhaps not the best and how they should be regulated. In fact, we all talk a lot about coherent regional development, and movements in this direction are urgent, but are also significantly too small or too slow. Recent legislative changes, to mention only the role of regional centres under the new scholarship law, have provided some important guidance for faster and more equitable development of all parts of Slovenia. More concrete measures should be launched to ensure equitable regional development. The establishment of regions is undoubtedly an important step in this direction. Just as the establishment of municipalities has brought important progress in all parts of Slovenia, the establishment of landscapes will undoubtedly ensure a certain degree of decentralisation and even development of our country. There may still be some skepticism about the sense of establishing another level of local self-government. I think that if we asked people about the effects of the establishment of local self-government in the form of municipalities, they have a very positive opinion about them. The financing of all parts of Slovenia through municipal budgets has enabled the establishment of emergency infrastructure for all places, including those that have been severely neglected in the larger municipalities in the previous country. But now it is needed, particularly for development reasons, slightly wider, i.e. a regional or provincial approach, because some municipalities are too small to effectively promote faster development in their own area. That's just the way life is. The Lands Act, the first law under consideration, is a statutory provincial law governing the legal status of the province, the labour areas of the province's tasks, the authorities of the province, the participation of the inhabitants in the decision-making, the tasks and organisation of the provincial administration, the property and the principles of the financing of the province, the statute of the province, the cooperation of the provinces with the municipalities, the State authorities and the mutual cooperation of the provinces. The draft law therefore lays down the foundations, a framework for the actual establishment of the provinces and their authorities. In general, in the Slovenian People's Party Group, we assess the proposed Law on Provinces as an appropriate and well-prepared law. Finally, it should be pointed out that the proposed law was produced through many different consultations and that many experts and other interested partners participated in the preparation. Legislation was preparing to find a broad consensus, and we are all aware of that. The final establishment of provincial legislation will require 2/3 of the majority in this National Assembly. The electoral system is also a very important part of any legislation or country, municipality or province. The electoral system law must ensure fair, fair and transparent elections. The electoral system must enable all regions or inhabitants of the province to be represented in the provincial council. However, it may depend on the electoral system and the manner in which the individual organs are elected, depending on how many options an individual party has or whether a particular individual is elected to one of the bodies. Last but not least, no electoral system, no way to vote is ideal, everyone has its drawbacks and advantages. With regard to the proposed law on elections to the provinces, we can say that the elections of the members of the provincial council are properly regulated. A proportional system is proposed, except for the elections of representatives of the Hungarian and Italian national communities elected in the majority elections. We believe that such a solution is appropriate. The bill also contains a provision on the electoral threshold for elections to the provincial councils. This threshold is set at 4%, corresponding to the comparative arrangement in the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, which also prescribes a 4% threshold for entry into the National Assembly. In the Slovenian People's Party's Group, we believe that this is an appropriate solution. For smaller customers, of course, such a solution may also be problematic, but something else should be highlighted. In cases where the electoral system is not limited to the electoral threshold, it would be easier for different so-called independent lists and independent candidates with a low percentage of votes to enter the provincial councils. This, however, does not seem to us well, especially because we believe that a large number of different sheets and then the appropriate composition of the provincial councils would not contribute to transparency, to transparency and not to the effective work of the provincial council. We therefore believe that the proposed electoral threshold is appropriate. The law also provides for a preferential vote, which, of course, gives the individual candidate the opportunity to be elected to the provincial council despite the less favourable place on the list. The determination of the condition for taking into account preferential votes is duly regulated. The proposed law on elections in the provinces also lays down all other necessary procedures and electoral tasks and the electoral bodies necessary for the normal conduct of the elections. That is why we also consider this law appropriate for further discussion. The third law in the package is the Law on the Financing of the Provinces. The province cannot operate without adequate financial resources. The search for autonomous funding sources for the provinces was also one of the most difficult tasks for law-makers. However, the funding system and its arrangements can have a decisive impact on the fair distribution of funds between all regions or on the even distribution of funds across Slovenia. In this regard, it is known that in the Slovenian People's Party we are persistent advocates of a fair distribution of funds in a way that ensures the equitable development of the whole country. The autonomy of the province or its status as a self-governing local community can only be achieved on condition that the province is also financially independent of its own revenue. The provisions with such a direction are also enshrined in the European Charter of Local Self-Government. In addition to the eligibility of local communities to adequate own financial resources and support for weaker communities in the form of financial imbalances, the European Charter stresses in particular that subsidies should not be strictly earmarked for local authorities. The proposed law on the financing of the provinces follows both the provisions of the European Charter of Local Self-Government and other European instruments. According to the proposal, the revenue from the part of the corporate tax, the share of income, excise duty and other resources, such as income from property, is income. However, it is also appropriate to compensate for the uneven influx of these revenues. This is important in terms of equitable development. If all the provinces were to be financed without compensation, those in more developed areas would be further developed and the rest would be increasingly lagging behind. That would be contrary to our goals. We also agree with the proposed criteria on the basis of which appropriate consumption will be calculated. At the end of this opinion, let me also touch on the assessments of the financial implications that the state budget will have with the adoption of provincial laws. According to the proposaler, the laws of financial implications beyond the planning would not have any effect, in general, the establishment of the provinces would be based on transfers of both employees and financial resources between the State, the provinces and the municipalities. We believe that this objective can be achieved, but good coordination and, above all, good operational implementation of the establishment of the provinces will be required. Any irregularities in the planning of the establishment of the provinces and in the actual reallocation of staff and financial resources will certainly have a significantly higher financial impact than expected. In the Group of the Slovenian People's Party, we are convinced that the proposed laws are suitable for further consideration. We will also listen to all of today's opinions and comments. Our objective is clear as soon as possible to adopt all the necessary provincial legislation, so we need to work towards consensus, as a key law in the area of provincial law will also require a two-thirds majority. In our view, however, it would be preposterous at this time to wait for the fourth law to determine the areas of the provinces. This, too, will certainly be necessary for us to accept. However, we hope that today's debate will be substantive in particular and that it will not go in the direction of a debate on who wants another additional province and that the seat must be in that or that place. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u17	Thank you. Mr. Mag. Franz Gnyardsich, he's not here. Mr Franc Jazbec will present the position of the Slovenian Democratic Party's Group.
ParlaMint-SI_2007-07-13-SDZ4-Redna-30.ana.u18	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Minister with colleagues, colleagues and colleagues! The laws on the provinces that we are going to discuss in detail today have been weighed through all the points of view and have heard too many concerns, and they have a long history. This issue has been addressed by all governments so far, unfortunately unsuccessful. In 1994, the National Assembly adopted the Law on Local Self-Government and established new municipalities, making it the first stage of local self-government. Thus, the powers of the State represented in the field by the administrative units and local authorities exercised by the municipalities were essentially separated. In the Constitution, however, we had written down that Slovenia could also have provinces. Since 1995, however, no government has gathered enough political power to undertake the so-called second stages of local self-government, i.e. the division of Slovenia into provinces. All attempts to establish the provinces so far have failed because of political will or rather said trouble. The current coalition has been tasked with carrying out a regional level of local self-government. Last year, the National Assembly also adopted an amendment to the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia. We have changed 121st, 140th, 143rd articles concerning the provinces and have clearly written in them that Slovenia has the provinces. The amendment of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia has been confirmed by 77 members of this House. However, only the Slovenian National Party and some individuals were against them. This step means a binding fact that we must establish provinces in Slovenia, not least because of more efficient EU funding. At the same time, so much support has given hope that this time we will bring the process to the end for the benefit of all citizens and citizens. If we focus only on the events we have witnessed in this mandate, that is, the events of the last two years, we can see that much has already been said on this subject, both positive and negative. The conclusion of all of this is the laws that are before us today and that we will discuss today. These laws were prepared by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for local self-government and regional policy and were then discussed within the coalition and discussed with opposition parties and, last but not least, there was much debate in the media. The debate has shown that the main dilemmas surrounding the so-called uniformity or duality of the election of the president of the province, the delegation of tasks, the financing of the province, the incompatibility of functions, the division of mandates within the provinces and the equal representation of the sexes. All the three laws that we are considering today represent, together with the so-called Landscape Establishment Act, a fundamental framework of provincial laws followed by others who will coordinate other laws with the landscape. The draft law on the provinces regulates the legal system components of the province in accordance with the constitutional principles of local self-government and the principles incorporated in the legal order of the Republic of Slovenia by the European Charter of Local Self-Government. The main content of the proposed province law is the functional component of the provincial self-government, which refers to the tasks of the province and the organisational component of the province's self-government, which defines the authorities of the province and the way in which decisions are taken in the province. The content of the law could also be described more simply by the fact that this law constitutes a basic framework for the functioning and organisation of the provinces. It also contains a procedure for transferring tasks to the jurisdiction of the provinces and ensuring conditions for the commencement of the work of the provinces. The provincial authorities are precisely identified, namely the provincial council, the president of the province and the council of municipalities and the provincial administration. The law stipulates that, within the framework of its competences, the province will carry out, in the interest of its population, matters of general interest which facilitate the development of the entire province, in a proposal for a law defined as local matters of wider importance and matters of regional importance. Local matters of wider importance are the tasks for which the province is generally responsible, already under the Constitution. According to the content, local matters are of wider importance, particularly in the areas of providing public services of regional importance and the tasks set out in the law as tasks under the jurisdiction of local self-government communities, i.e. municipalities or provinces, are of wider importance by their nature, since they are common areas of several municipalities and exceed their financial capacity. The tasks of regional importance are in the areas of regional policy on environment and space, transport, economy, education and sport, culture, health and social. The law also ensures a balanced transfer of tasks from country to country. As from 1 January 2009, administrative tasks under national jurisdiction will be transferred to the provinces. In the second phase, the ministries responsible for individual areas of administrative tasks should monitor their work for two years after the establishment of the provinces and, based on an analysis, propose solutions within three years at the latest with regard to the transfer of the performance of tasks from the administrative units to the provinces. The bill provides the province with legal status, legal entities of public law and, in principle, property and financial autonomy. This means that the provinces have assets and their own funding resources, which they manage independently on the basis of the provincial budget. The second law we are discussing today is the Land Financing Act. This law has raised the greatest concerns during the coordination period and has received special attention. The first drafts were amended several times after this and the final funding formula was intended to be the complete autonomy of the provinces, which is the meaning of this [[formula]] It is. The financing of the landscapes is divided into three main sources: firstly, development funding, which includes 25% of the corporate income tax paid by debtors established in the area of the province in the past year. Secondly, adequate spending financing covers 20% of income, 20% of excise duty on alcohol and alcohol products, 5% of excise duty on energy and electricity and 5% of excise duty on tobacco products. Thirdly, resources that are revenue from public services, property, state budget and European resources, as well as part of concession fees, water reimbursement and reimbursement for the use of roads. The appropriate consumption formula is the calculation of the ratio between the area of the individual province per capita and the total area of the country per capita; the ratio of the proportion of the population aged between 15 and 24 years in the whole population of the country and the share in the country; the ratio between the proportion of the population aged 65 and over in the entire population of the country and the share in the country. The province's development index, which is the ratio between the province's development threat index and the weighted, arithmetic mean of the country's development hazard index; the average and the number of inhabitants of the province. This formula should ensure the complete autonomy of the provinces to which we also tend. The draft law also lays down the subject of the legal basis and the criteria for co-financing the tasks of the programmes and investments of the provinces. Co-financing from the State budget follows in principle a wider public benefit and promotion of coherent regional development. The third law is the Law on Elections to the Provinces. The purpose of the law is to put the system of elections into the authorities of the provinces, this is in the provincial council. The law thus regulates electoral law, electoral system, electoral bodies, general, early and alternate elections, candidateing, voting and electoral outcomes, and the protection of electoral law. The transitional and final provisions specifically regulate the first elections to the provincial councils. The bill is based on the principles of local self-government based on the constitutional arrangements of local self-government in the Republic of Slovenia and the principles of the European Charter on Local Self-Government. If we look at it in general, all legislation prepared is based on a single system, that is on the transfer of the jurisdiction of the country to the provinces and thus on the gradual abolition of administrative units. This principle makes it possible for the administration not to expand significantly, and this is also the basis for a second degree of local self-government not to undermine the functioning of the State and local bureaucracy. Today's debate will, I hope, help bring many, new views or dispel all the concerns we have so far. There are parliamentary holidays ahead of us, a period in which time will run out for a 60-day public debate by the municipalities on the so-called fourth law. Thus, early autumn will also be possible to harmonise the Law on the Establishment of Landscapes before the adoption of these laws in other readings, both on the home working body and on the prey. Members of the Slovenian Democratic Party will be pleased to hear the debate, at least in the part that will be constructive, we will also support any amendments to improve the laws, if necessary. For we want the laws to have such content that it will allow for a smooth, cheap operation and action for the benefit of the provinces and their inhabitants. It follows from everything we say that the Members of the Slovenian Democratic Party will support the draft laws as suitable for further consideration. Thank you very much.
